01. UiPath Principal Applied Scientist Loop: Format, ML System Design Focus, Research Presentation and the Principal Bar

How to read this chapter. You asked for ML system design, so this chapter centres on it. It explains where the ML system design round sits in the UiPath Principal Applied Scientist loop, what the principal-level bar looks like in that round, and the process-level questions you are likely to get there. The research presentation and values rounds are covered only as far as they connect to system design. Diagrams are plain-text block diagrams, so they render the same in Markdown and in a PDF export.

Tagging system. Every question carries one of two tags:

  • [Reported] [n]: a candidate report, question bank or official page links this question, or this round type, to UiPath. The number points to the References list.
  • [Likely]: an inference from the role, the level and UiPath's AI products (agentic automation, Document Understanding, Communications Mining, Autopilot, the AI Trust Layer, computer vision for UI automation). No candidate has reported it.
  • Indented sub-bullets are follow-up probes an interviewer may use to push a strong first answer toward the principal bar.

Research limits (please read). This run could not do any live research. The shared web-search budget for this workflow was already used up before the chapter started. Direct fetches of glassdoor.com, teamblind.com and uipath.com also failed, because the egress proxy rejected them and DNS lookups for WebFetch failed. So no question below is tagged [Reported]: none could be checked against a candidate write-up, and this chapter does not invent reports. Every question is [Likely]. The format notes describe the usual principal-scientist loop at enterprise AI companies of UiPath's size and shape; they are not a confirmed UiPath process. Confirm round names, durations and the presentation format with your recruiter. If you need sourced reports, a follow-up run with search budget can upgrade tags from [Likely] to [Reported].

Review pass (2026-10-14). An adversarial review re-checked this chapter. Web search was again unavailable (the shared search budget was used up). Direct fetches of 12 candidate source pages were tried: UiPath careers, hiring process, blog, product and documentation pages, Glassdoor, Blind, Levels.fyi and Interview Query. Every fetch failed with a DNS error or a proxy 403, so no source could be verified and no question was upgraded to [Reported]. The review removed two generic leadership questions that did not test system design (they are covered in chapter 06). It added 21 [Likely] principal-level design prompts on gaps: capacity and cost estimation, idempotent agent actions, process versioning, agent tracing, computer-use evaluation harnesses, multi-agent attribution, data flywheels and tenant isolation, synthetic document data, rule-validated extraction, PII redaction, cross-source retrieval, red-teaming, model retirement, cost attribution, judge drift, delayed-outcome experiments, on-device inference, caching and platform consolidation.

Format notes

Typical loop shape (inferred, not confirmed)

Stage Typical duration Interviewer Status Principal bar assessed
Recruiter screen 30 min Recruiter Likely Scope match (agents, document AI, comms mining, CV for UI), level calibration, compensation
Hiring manager screen 45-60 min Director or Head of AI/ML Likely Track record of research shipped into product; ability to set direction for a team
Technical screen 45-60 min Senior or staff scientist Likely ML/LLM depth plus a short design discussion or ML coding
Research / technical presentation 60 min (about 30-40 min talk plus Q&A) Panel of scientists, engineers and a product lead Likely Depth, rigour, and whether you can turn research into a product system
ML system design 60 min Principal scientist or architect Likely End-to-end design of an LLM or agentic system with evaluation, safety, cost and rollout
ML depth / applied science 60 min Staff or principal scientist Likely Modelling choices, evaluation methodology, failure analysis
Coding (ML or general) 45-60 min Engineer or scientist Likely Clean, working code; less weight than at junior levels
Cross-functional / product 45 min PM or engineering leader Likely Turning customer automation problems into ML roadmaps
Values / leadership (Humble, Bold, Immersed, Fast) 45 min Senior leader Likely Influence without authority, judgement, pace, ownership

Where ML system design shows up

  Recruiter ──> HM screen ──> Tech screen ──> ONSITE LOOP ──> Debrief / calibration
                    │               │               │
                    │               │               ├── Research presentation  (design Q&A on YOUR system)
                    │               │               ├── ML system design       (design a NEW UiPath-shaped system)
                    │               │               ├── ML depth               (evaluation / modelling probes)
                    │               │               ├── Coding
                    │               │               ├── Product / cross-functional (design trade-offs vs roadmap)
                    │               │               └── Values / leadership
                    │               └── short design discussion likely
                    └── "how would you architect X at UiPath" likely

System design probably comes up in three places, not one. You may be asked to (a) re-architect your own past system during presentation Q&A, (b) design a new system in the dedicated round, and (c) defend design trade-offs against product constraints in the cross-functional round.

Canvas for the design round (a whiteboard layout, not an answer)

Use this layout to structure any prompt below, so the interviewer can see every principal-level concern on the board.

 ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
 │ 1. PROBLEM & SLOs: user, task, success metric, latency, cost/run, accuracy    │
 ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
 │ 2. DATA: sources (docs, emails, UI screens, logs), labels, PII, tenant bounds │
 ├───────────────┬───────────────────────┬───────────────────────┬──────────────┤
 │ 3. INGEST /   │ 4. MODEL LAYER        │ 5. ORCHESTRATION /    │ 6. HUMAN-IN- │
 │    PREPROCESS │   (LLM, specialist    │    AGENT RUNTIME      │    THE-LOOP  │
 │  OCR, parse,  │    models, retrieval, │  tools, robots, APIs, │  validation, │
 │  chunk, embed │    routing)           │  guardrails, retries  │  escalation  │
 ├───────────────┴───────────────────────┴───────────────────────┴──────────────┤
 │ 7. EVALUATION: offline sets, LLM-as-judge, task success, human review rate    │
 ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
 │ 8. TRUST & GOVERNANCE: PII masking, audit, policy, model choice per tenant    │
 ├──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
 │ 9. DEPLOY & OPERATE: cloud/on-prem, rollout, monitoring, drift, feedback loop │
 └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

What the principal bar likely means in design rounds

  • Problem framing before architecture. You turn a vague automation goal into measurable SLOs: straight-through processing rate, human review rate, cost per document or per agent run, latency, and error cost.
  • Mix of deterministic and probabilistic parts. UiPath's core is deterministic RPA robots. A principal candidate is expected to decide what belongs in a model, what belongs in a rule or a robot, and where people approve or correct the result.
  • Evaluation as a first-class part of the system. Benchmarks, regression suites, LLM-as-judge calibration, and online metrics that tie back to customer ROI.
  • Enterprise constraints. Multi-tenant isolation, PII and data residency, on-prem and air-gapped deployments, customer choice of model provider, and auditability.
  • Platform thinking. You design a reusable capability (for example, one extraction or agent-evaluation framework) that many product teams and customers can use, not a one-off model.
  • Technical leadership. You can explain the roadmap, the staffing, what you would build first, and how you would know to stop or pivot.

Questions

A. Process and expectations for the design round

  1. [Likely] Walk me through how you approach a 60-minute ML system design interview, and what you clarify in the first ten minutes.
    • Which requirements would you refuse to assume, and ask about instead?
    • How do you decide when to go deep on one component versus staying broad?
  2. [Likely] At principal level, what do you think separates a good system design answer from a great one?
    • Can you give an example from your own work where the design choice mattered more than the model choice?
  3. [Likely] How would you design a system when the interviewer gives you no numbers? What numbers would you estimate yourself?
    • How would you size cost per agent run or per document for an enterprise customer processing 1M documents a month?
  4. [Likely] How do you draw the boundary between what an LLM should do and what a deterministic robot, rule or API call should do?
    • Give a case where you moved logic out of a model and into deterministic code. What did it buy you?
  5. [Likely] What does "production ready" mean for an ML system you own, and what checklist would you use before launch?
    • Who signs off, and on what evidence?
  6. [Likely] How do you present trade-offs to an interviewer who keeps changing the constraints mid-design?
    • What would you re-do on the board if latency suddenly had to be 10x lower?
  7. [Likely] Do a back-of-envelope estimate on the board: 50,000 agent runs a day, about 12 LLM steps per run, about 3,000 input tokens per step. What are tokens per day, peak requests per second and monthly cost at two price tiers?
    • Which assumption dominates the answer, and how would you check it with the customer?
  8. [Likely] In the last ten minutes the interviewer asks, "What breaks first at 10x scale?" How do you structure that answer for the system on your board?

B. Agentic automation system design (UiPath-shaped prompts)

  1. [Likely] Design an agentic automation platform that lets enterprise users build agents that call tools, trigger RPA robots and hand off to people.
    • How do agents, robots and people share state across a long-running process?
    • Where do you put guardrails, and what happens when a tool call fails halfway through?
  2. [Likely] Design an orchestration layer that coordinates several agents and robots across a multi-step business process (for example, order-to-cash).
  • How do you make the process recoverable and auditable after a crash?
  • How do you stop an agent from looping forever or spending too much?
  1. [Likely] Design an evaluation system for enterprise agents whose prompts, tools and underlying LLMs change often.
  • How do you build test sets when customer data is private and differs per tenant?
  • How would you calibrate an LLM-as-judge against human labels, and what agreement level would you accept?
  1. [Likely] Design a system that turns a natural-language description of a task into a working automation workflow.
    • How do you check the generated workflow before it runs on production systems?
    • What is your fallback when generation confidence is low?
  2. [Likely] Design an agent that operates desktop and web applications from screenshots, without selectors or APIs.
    • How do you combine a vision-language model with UI element detection and existing selectors?
    • How do you measure task success, and how do you make it safe to run against live systems?
  3. [Likely] Design a self-healing system for UI automations that break when a target application's interface changes.
    • How do you detect breakage before customers report it?
    • When should the system repair the automation itself, and when should it ask a developer?
  4. [Likely] Design a tool-selection and routing layer for an agent that has hundreds of enterprise connectors available.
    • How do you keep the prompt small while keeping recall high on the right tool?
    • How would you evaluate routing errors separately from execution errors?
  5. [Likely] Design memory and context management for long-running agents that work on a case over days.
    • What do you store, what do you summarise, and what must never be stored?
  6. [Likely] How would you design human-in-the-loop escalation so agents ask for help at the right rate?
    • How do you set confidence thresholds per action type and per customer?
    • How do reviewer corrections flow back into the system?
  7. [Likely] Design a cost-and-latency-aware model router that picks between small, large, hosted and self-hosted models per step.
    • What signals decide the route, and how do you stop quality from silently dropping?
  8. [Likely] Design idempotency and compensation for agent actions that write to systems of record such as an ERP or CRM.
    • How do you handle a tool call that timed out but may have succeeded?
    • When would you use a saga with compensating actions instead of a human approval step?
  9. [Likely] Design a long-running process orchestrator in which modelled process steps mix agent steps, robot steps and human tasks.
    • How do you ship a new process version while thousands of instances of the old one are still running?
  10. [Likely] Design tracing and observability for agent runs.
    • What goes into a span, and how do you make a non-deterministic LLM run replayable for debugging?
    • How do you strip PII from traces and still keep them useful?
  11. [Likely] Design an evaluation harness for computer-use agents that work in enterprise applications (ERP, CRM, legacy desktop apps).
    • How do you build sandboxed replicas, reset their state between runs and score partial task completion?
  12. [Likely] Design a multi-agent system where a planner hands work to specialist agents.
    • When is multi-agent worse than one agent with tools, and how do you attribute a failure to the right agent?
  13. [Likely] Design a data flywheel that turns agent traces, reviewer corrections and business outcomes into evaluation and training data.
    • How do you guarantee one tenant's data never improves, or leaks into, another tenant's model?

C. Document AI and communications system design

  1. [Likely] Design an intelligent document processing system that extracts fields from invoices, contracts and forms across many layouts and languages.
    • Where do OCR, layout models, specialised extractors and LLMs each fit?
    • How do you produce calibrated field-level confidence for straight-through processing?
  2. [Likely] Design a system that lets a customer build a new document extractor with ten labelled examples.
    • How do you choose between prompting, few-shot retrieval and fine-tuning?
    • How do you tell the customer whether the extractor is good enough to go live?
  3. [Likely] Design a document classification and splitting pipeline for multi-document PDFs that arrive in one email.
    • How do you handle page-order errors and mixed scans?
  4. [Likely] Design a communications mining system that finds intents, entities and sentiment in high-volume enterprise email and tickets, then triggers automations.
    • How do you discover new intents nobody labelled yet?
    • How would you handle taxonomy drift when the business changes?
  5. [Likely] Design an active learning loop for document and message models where every label costs expert time.
    • Which sampling strategy would you use, and how would you show it beats random labelling?
  6. [Likely] Design a table extraction system for complex financial documents with nested and multi-page tables.
    • How do you evaluate table structure accuracy as well as cell values?
  7. [Likely] How would you decide whether a document understanding product should use one general foundation model or many task-specific models?
    • What would the cost, latency and accuracy data need to show to change your mind?
  8. [Likely] Design a synthetic data pipeline (layouts, handwriting, poor scans) to bootstrap extractors for a new document type or language.
    • How do you show that synthetic data helps on the real distribution and is not just inflating offline scores?
  9. [Likely] Design schema-constrained LLM extraction with business-rule validation (line items sum to the total, dates are consistent, vendor matches master data).
    • How should rule violations change field-level confidence and routing to human review?
  10. [Likely] Design support for handwriting, low-resource languages and right-to-left scripts in a document understanding product.
    • How do you decide which languages get dedicated models and which rely on a general model?
  11. [Likely] Design a PII detection and redaction model that runs before any document or email reaches an external LLM.
    • How do you measure its recall, and what happens to a request when recall is uncertain?

D. Retrieval, grounding and enterprise knowledge

  1. [Likely] Design a retrieval-augmented system that answers questions over a customer's processes, documents and automation logs.
    • How do you enforce per-user access rights during retrieval?
    • How do you measure groundedness and catch answers that sound plausible but are wrong?
  2. [Likely] Design an assistant that helps automation developers write and debug workflows inside the studio IDE.
    • What context do you retrieve from the project, and how do you keep it fresh?
    • How do you measure developer productivity gains rather than suggestion acceptance alone?
  3. [Likely] Design a process mining plus ML system that recommends which processes a customer should automate next.
    • How do you estimate automation ROI from event logs and task-mining data?
    • How do you avoid recommending processes that are frequent but too variable to automate?
  4. [Likely] Design an embedding and indexing service that many product teams share.
    • How do you version embeddings when the model changes, without re-indexing every tenant at once?
  5. [Likely] Design question answering over semi-structured enterprise sources (spreadsheets, ticket histories, process definitions) where the answer needs a join across sources.
    • When do you use text-to-SQL or tool calls instead of vector retrieval?

E. Trust, safety, governance and enterprise deployment

  1. [Likely] Design an AI trust layer that sits between UiPath products and any LLM provider.
    • Where do PII masking, prompt-injection defence, logging and policy enforcement run?
    • How do you prove to an auditor what the model saw and what it did?
  2. [Likely] How would you defend an agent that reads untrusted emails and documents against prompt injection that tries to trigger robot actions?
    • Which actions need human approval no matter what the model's confidence is?
  3. [Likely] Design an ML system that must run in the cloud, on-prem and in air-gapped customer environments.
    • What changes in model choice, updates and monitoring for each?
  4. [Likely] How would you support customers who bring their own LLM, when your evaluation and prompts were tuned on a different model?
    • How do you certify a new model and roll it out per tenant?
  5. [Likely] Design monitoring for model drift and quality regression when you cannot look at customer data.
    • Which privacy-preserving signals would you rely on?
  6. [Likely] How would you design data governance for training on customer data that some tenants allow and others forbid?
    • How would you trace which data trained which model version?
  7. [Likely] Design an audit and explainability feature for an agent that made a financial decision inside an automated process.
  8. [Likely] Design a red-teaming and guardrail evaluation programme for an agent platform.
    • How do you weigh the cost of guardrail false positives (blocked legitimate work) against missed attacks?
  9. [Likely] An LLM provider will retire a model that customers' production agents depend on in 90 days. Design the migration process.
    • How do you find every affected agent, re-certify behaviour and handle customers who refuse to move?
  10. [Likely] Design per-tenant cost attribution and budget enforcement for LLM usage across several products.

F. Evaluation, experimentation and rollout

  1. [Likely] How would you build an offline benchmark for agentic tasks that predicts customer-facing success?
    • How do you stop the benchmark from going stale or leaking into training?
  2. [Likely] Design an A/B or staged rollout for a new extraction or agent model when each customer has a small, different workload.
    • What would you do when the aggregate metric improves but a large customer gets worse?
  3. [Likely] Which online metrics would you put on the executive dashboard for an agent product, and which would you keep internal?
  4. [Likely] How do you set and defend a "good enough to automate" threshold with a customer whose error tolerance differs from yours?
  5. [Likely] Design a regression testing pipeline that runs every time a prompt, tool schema or model version changes.
    • How do you handle non-determinism in LLM outputs during testing?
  6. [Likely] How would you measure whether human reviewers are a reliable gold standard?
  7. [Likely] Your LLM-as-judge model is upgraded by its provider. How would you detect that its scores have shifted, and what would you do about historical comparisons?
  8. [Likely] Design an online experiment for an agent whose true outcome (for example, an invoice paid correctly) is known only weeks later.
    • Which proxy metrics would you use, and how would you validate them against the delayed outcome?

G. Scaling, efficiency and serving

  1. [Likely] Design LLM serving for millions of short agent steps a day with strict cost targets.
    • Where do caching, batching, distillation and quantisation help, and where do they hurt?
  2. [Likely] How would you distil a large model into a small specialised model for a high-volume document or classification task?
    • How do you decide the small model is safe to replace the large one?
  3. [Likely] Design a fine-tuning platform that adapts base models per customer without a full model copy per tenant.
    • How would you serve many adapters efficiently?
  4. [Likely] How do you plan GPU capacity for a product whose usage is spiky and tied to customers' business hours?
  5. [Likely] What would you change in your design if per-request latency must drop below one second for an attended (user-facing) automation?
  6. [Likely] Design inference for a screen-understanding model that must run on customer desktops with CPU only, for attended robots.
    • What do you trade among model size, accuracy and startup time, and how do you ship model updates?
  7. [Likely] Design caching for agent steps (prompt-prefix caching, semantic caching of LLM answers and tool results).
    • How do you prevent stale answers and cross-tenant cache hits?

H. Research presentation: design Q&A on your own system

  1. [Likely] Present a system you designed end to end. What was the hardest design decision, and what did you reject?
    • If you rebuilt it today with current LLMs, what would you delete?
  2. [Likely] Draw the architecture of your presented system and mark where it failed in production.
    • How did you find out, and what did you change in the design?
  3. [Likely] How did you go from a research result to something a product team could own and operate?
    • What did you hand over: code, eval suite, runbooks, people?
  4. [Likely] Which baseline did you compare against, and why should we believe the comparison was fair?
  5. [Likely] What is the single number that best describes the impact of your system, and how was it measured?
  6. [Likely] How would your presented system map onto a UiPath product such as agents, document understanding or communications mining?
    • What would need to change for multi-tenant enterprise deployment?
  7. [Likely] What part of your presented work would you publish, and what would you keep as product advantage?

I. Principal-level scope and technical leadership in design

  1. [Likely] Describe a time you set the technical direction for an ML platform used by several teams.
    • How did you get teams that did not report to you to adopt it?
  2. [Likely] How would you write a 12-month applied science roadmap for agentic automation at UiPath?
    • What would you build, buy or partner on, given fast-moving foundation models?
  3. [Likely] How do you decide when to invest in a custom model versus using a frontier API?
    • What would trigger you to reverse that decision?
  4. [Likely] Tell me about a design review where you blocked or significantly changed a launch.
    • How did you handle the disagreement with product or engineering?
  5. [Likely] How do you review another scientist's system design, and what do you look for first?
  6. [Likely] Describe a system you designed that turned out to be the wrong bet. How early did you notice, and what did you do?
  7. [Likely] You inherit three teams, each with its own document extraction stack. Design the consolidation plan and the metrics that tell you it worked.
    • What would you keep separate on purpose?

J. Values lens on design decisions (Humble, Bold, Immersed, Fast)

  1. [Likely] Humble: Tell me about a design of yours that a junior colleague or a customer improved.
    • What did you change in how you run design reviews afterwards?
  2. [Likely] Humble: When did your evaluation show that your favourite approach was worse than a simple baseline?
  3. [Likely] Bold: Describe a time you proposed an architecture that most people thought was too ambitious.
    • How did you reduce the risk while keeping the ambition?
  4. [Likely] Bold: What design bet would you make for UiPath's agentic platform that others might disagree with?
  5. [Likely] Immersed: How did you learn the customer's real workflow before designing an ML system for it?
    • Have you sat with operators or reviewers who used your system? What did you learn?
  6. [Likely] Immersed: Tell me about a design detail you only got right because you read the raw data or logs yourself.
  7. [Likely] Fast: Describe a time you shipped a smaller version of a system early to learn quickly.
    • What did you deliberately leave out of v1, and how did you protect quality?
  8. [Likely] Fast: How do you keep moving fast when the foundation models underneath your system change every few months?

K. Questions you should be ready to ask (reverse questions)

  1. [Likely] What does the ML system design round expect from a principal candidate compared with a staff candidate?
  2. [Likely] Is the research presentation on my own work, a given topic, or a take-home design?
  3. [Likely] Which UiPath product area (agents, document AI, communications mining, CV for UI automation, process intelligence) would this role design for first?
  4. [Likely] How do applied scientists and platform engineers share ownership of production systems here?
  5. [Likely] How does UiPath evaluate agents today, and what is the biggest gap?
  6. [Likely] What constraints from on-prem and regulated customers most shape model and architecture choices?

References

02. Research Presentation Round: Presenting Your ML System, and the Design Probes Principal Scientists Get Afterwards

How to read this chapter. You asked for ML system design, so this chapter treats the research or job-talk presentation as a system design round you lead yourself. It covers how to structure the talk around architecture diagrams, and the probing questions a panel is likely to ask about your system's design, evaluation, deployment and transfer to UiPath products. General research-talk questions (publication history, theory) are included only where they lead into design.

Tagging system (the same as Chapter 01):

  • [Reported] [n]: a candidate report, question bank or official page links the question, or this round type, to UiPath. The number points to the References list.
  • [Likely]: an inference from the role, the principal level and UiPath's AI products (agentic automation, Document Understanding, Communications Mining, Autopilot, the AI Trust Layer, computer vision for UI automation, process mining). No candidate has reported it.
  • Indented sub-bullets are follow-up probes, phrased as questions.

Research limits (please read). Two runs have now tried to source this chapter, and neither could do live research. In the first run the workflow's shared web-search budget (200 searches per turn) was already used up. In the second (review) run the search budget was again exhausted, and direct fetches of glassdoor.com, teamblind.com, uipath.com, docs.uipath.com and arxiv.org all failed with DNS errors (ENOTFOUND). No cited URL could be verified, and no new candidate-reported question could be found. So no question below is tagged [Reported]. Every question is [Likely], and the format notes describe how principal-scientist job talks usually run at enterprise AI companies; they are not a confirmed UiPath process. Ask your recruiter for the presentation format, the length and who sits on the panel. A follow-up run with working search could upgrade tags to [Reported].

Review changes. The review run removed generic and duplicate questions (for example, separate PII, mentoring and "what would you do differently" questions that repeated others). It also added principal-level stress tests that probe specific arrows on your architecture diagram (Section L) and moved general agent-design questions that Chapter 04 already covers out of this chapter.

Format notes (inferred, not confirmed)

Item Typical for principal applied scientist job talks Status
Duration 60 min: about 30-40 min talk, 20-30 min Q&A; some companies interrupt during the talk Likely
Topic Your own past work, usually one or two projects that reached production; sometimes a "vision for X at our company" section Likely
Audience Panel of 3-8: scientists (staff/principal), ML or platform engineers, a product lead, often the hiring manager Likely
Materials Slides shared in advance or presented live; confidential details abstracted Likely
Debrief weight Often a heavy signal, because every panel member sees the same material Likely

What the principal bar likely assesses in this round

  • Depth and rigour. Sound baselines, ablations and error analysis; you know where the method breaks.
  • Research-to-product conversion. A clear path from a result to a system that others operate, with SLOs, costs and monitoring.
  • System design ownership. You can draw the architecture, defend each box and say what you would change today.
  • Scope and influence. The work changed a roadmap, a platform or several teams, not only one model.
  • Communication. Mixed audiences (scientists, engineers, product) all follow the talk.
  • Transfer. You can map the work onto UiPath problems: agents that call robots and tools, document and message understanding, UI vision, enterprise trust and governance.

Suggested talk structure built around system diagrams

 ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
 │ 1. Problem & business stakes      (2-3 min)  who hurt, how much, SLOs     │
 │ 2. Why it was hard                (3 min)    data, scale, constraints      │
 │ 3. SYSTEM ARCHITECTURE DIAGRAM    (5-7 min)  boxes, data flow, owners      │
 │ 4. Key technical idea(s)          (8-10 min) the research contribution     │
 │ 5. Evaluation                     (5 min)    offline -> online -> business │
 │ 6. Production & failures          (4 min)    what broke, what you changed  │
 │ 7. Impact & scope                 (2 min)    metrics, teams, roadmap       │
 │ 8. If I did it at UiPath          (3 min)    mapping + next 12 months      │
 └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

One architecture slide the panel will probe (template)

  Data sources ──> Ingest / labelling ──> Training & eval ──> Model registry
       │                  │                     │                   │
       │            (active learning)     (offline bench)           v
       │                  ^                                   Serving / agent runtime ──> Robots / tools / APIs
       │                  │                                         │
       └──── feedback ────┴──── human review / corrections <────────┤
                                                                    v
                                         Monitoring: quality, drift, cost, latency, audit log

Expect probes at every arrow: who owns it, what fails there, how you measure it, and what it costs.

Second slide worth preparing: evaluation and feedback loop

  Production traffic ──> sampler (stratified by tenant, doc type, confidence)
          │                          │
          │                          v
          │                 human labels / reviewer corrections ──> golden set (versioned, frozen)
          │                          │                                    │
          v                          v                                    v
  shadow / canary model ──> offline + online metrics ──> release gate (thresholds, CIs, guardrails)
                                     │                                    │
                                     └──── error clusters ──> data / model / prompt fix ──┘

Panels at this level often skip the model and ask about this loop: how the sample is drawn, how reviewer corrections are de-biased, and what stops a release.

Questions

A. Framing and problem selection

  1. [Likely] Why did you choose this project for your presentation, and what does it show about you at principal level?
    • What other project did you consider presenting, and why not that one?
  2. [Likely] What was the business problem in one sentence, and who measured success?
    • What SLOs (accuracy, latency, cost per request, review rate) did you commit to?
  3. [Likely] How did you decide this problem needed research rather than an off-the-shelf model?
    • What did the simplest baseline achieve, and how far was it from the target?
  4. [Likely] What constraints (data access, privacy, latency, hardware, regulation) shaped the design most?
    • Which constraint did you push back on, and did you win?

B. Architecture walk-through

  1. [Likely] Walk us through the architecture diagram box by box. Which box did you design yourself?
    • Which box took the most engineering time, and was that expected?
  2. [Likely] Where is the single point of failure in this design?
    • What happens to users when that component goes down?
  3. [Likely] Which parts are learned models and which are deterministic code or rules, and why did you split them that way?
    • Did you ever move logic from a model into code, or the reverse?
  4. [Likely] How does data flow from raw input to the final action, and where is it stored along the way?
    • Where does personally identifiable information appear, and how is it protected?
  5. [Likely] What alternative architectures did you reject, and what evidence decided it?
  6. [Likely] How did the system handle inputs far outside the training distribution?
    • How did it signal low confidence to the downstream system or user?
  7. [Likely] What were the interfaces between your system and the teams upstream and downstream?
    • Who could change those interfaces, and how did you version them?
  8. [Likely] Draw how the architecture would look if traffic grew 100x. What breaks first?
  9. [Likely] How did you handle retries, idempotency and partial failure when the model triggered actions in other systems?

C. Modelling and the core technical contribution

  1. [Likely] What is the one technical idea in this work that someone else would not have tried?
    • Is it new in the literature, or new in how you applied it?
  2. [Likely] Why this model family? What would a large general LLM have done on the same task?
    • Did you test prompting or fine-tuning a foundation model as a baseline?
  3. [Likely] Walk us through the loss function or training objective, and why it fits the business metric.
  4. [Likely] Which ablation surprised you most?
    • Which component could we remove with almost no loss?
  5. [Likely] How did you get labels, and how noisy were they?
    • How did you measure label quality and annotator agreement?
  6. [Likely] How much labelled data did you need, and how would that change for a new customer or domain?
    • Did you use active learning, weak supervision or synthetic data? How did you validate it?
  7. [Likely] If you had to make the model ten times cheaper to serve, what would you do?
    • Distillation, quantisation, caching, smaller models per step: which did you try?

D. Evaluation rigour

  1. [Likely] What was your offline evaluation set, and how do you know it represented production traffic?
    • How often was it refreshed, and how did you stop leakage into training?
  2. [Likely] Which baselines did you compare against, and why should we believe the comparison was fair?
    • Did baselines get the same tuning budget as your method?
  3. [Likely] Report the confidence interval or variance on your headline number.
    • Was the improvement statistically and practically significant?
    • How much of the gain survives a change of seed, data ordering or hyperparameters?
  4. [Likely] How did offline gains translate to online metrics, and where did they not?
  5. [Likely] How did you evaluate the parts of the output that have no single correct answer (for example, generated text or agent plans)?
    • If you used an LLM as a judge, how did you calibrate it against humans?
  6. [Likely] Show us the error analysis. What are the top three failure categories?
    • Which failure is most costly to the customer, rather than most frequent?
  7. [Likely] How did you evaluate fairness or performance across subgroups, languages or customers?
  8. [Likely] What did the A/B test or staged rollout look like, and what guardrail metrics did you watch?
    • What would have made you stop the rollout?

E. Production, operations and failures

  1. [Likely] What went wrong after launch that your offline evaluation did not predict?
    • How did you detect it, and how long did it take?
  2. [Likely] How did you monitor model quality without ground-truth labels in production?
  3. [Likely] How did you detect and handle data drift or concept drift?
    • Did you retrain on a schedule or on a trigger?
  4. [Likely] What was the on-call or ownership model after launch, and who got paged?
  5. [Likely] What did the system cost to run per month, and how did that compare to the value it created?
  6. [Likely] How did you handle a rollback, and how fast could you do it?
  7. [Likely] What did you hand over to the product or platform team: code, evaluation suite, runbooks, people?
    • Is the system still running without you?

F. Agentic and LLM-system follow-ups

  1. [Likely] If you rebuilt this system today with current LLMs and agents, what would you delete and what would you keep?
  2. [Likely] How would you turn this system into an agent that calls tools, triggers robots and asks people for help?
    • Which actions would always need human approval?
  3. [Likely] How would you evaluate an agent version of your system whose prompts, tools and underlying model change often?
    • How would you build a regression suite that runs on every change?
  4. [Likely] How would you route between small, large, hosted and self-hosted models for different steps?
    • How would you stop quality dropping silently when the router changes?
  5. [Likely] What part of your system would you replace with a vision-language model reading screens, and what risk does that add?

G. Enterprise constraints and trust

  1. [Likely] How would your system work for a multi-tenant enterprise product where each customer's data must stay isolated?
  2. [Likely] How would you deploy this on-prem or in an air-gapped customer environment?
    • How would you update models and monitor quality there?
  3. [Likely] How would the design change if customers bring their own LLM provider?
  4. [Likely] How would you explain a decision your system made to an auditor or a regulator?
    • What would the audit log contain?
  5. [Likely] How would you train or improve the model when some customers allow their data to be used and others forbid it?

H. Mapping your work to UiPath products

  1. [Likely] Which UiPath product area would benefit most from your work: agents, document understanding, communications mining, UI automation or process mining?
    • What is the first experiment you would run there in your first 90 days?
  2. [Likely] How would your extraction or classification approach handle invoices, contracts and forms across many layouts and languages?
    • How would it produce field-level confidence for straight-through processing?
  3. [Likely] How would your approach help customers build a new extractor or classifier with only a handful of examples?
  4. [Likely] How would your work apply to a system that finds intents in high-volume email and then triggers automations?
    • How would you handle new intents nobody labelled?
  5. [Likely] How would your work apply to UI automations that break when the target application changes?
  6. [Likely] How would you decide whether UiPath should build this capability, buy it, or rely on a foundation-model provider?
  7. [Likely] What would make your approach a mistake for UiPath's customers?

I. Scope, influence and technical leadership

  1. [Likely] What was your exact role, and what did others on the team do?
    • Which decisions would not have happened without you?
  2. [Likely] How many teams adopted this work, and how did you get teams that did not report to you to use it?
  3. [Likely] Describe a disagreement about this design with product or engineering. How was it resolved?
  4. [Likely] What did this project change in your organisation's roadmap or platform?
  5. [Likely] How did you balance publication goals with product deadlines on this project?
    • What did you publish, and what did you keep as product advantage?
  6. [Likely] If you led this at UiPath, what would your 12-month roadmap look like, and how would you staff it?
  7. [Likely] What would make you stop or pivot this line of work?

J. Reflection and judgement

  1. [Likely] What is the weakest part of your work, and how would a sceptical reviewer attack it?
  2. [Likely] Which result in your talk do you trust least?
  3. [Likely] Tell us about a design decision where you were wrong and a junior colleague or customer was right. (Humble)
  4. [Likely] What was the boldest bet in this project, and how did you reduce its risk? (Bold)
  5. [Likely] What detail did you only get right because you looked at raw data or sat with users? (Immersed)
  6. [Likely] What did you leave out of version one to ship faster, and did it hurt later? (Fast)

K. Presentation mechanics and clarification probes

  1. [Likely] Can you go back to the architecture slide and explain the arrow between these two components?
  2. [Likely] What does this metric mean in customer terms, for example hours saved or documents processed without review?
    • Can you explain the core idea to the product manager on the panel in two minutes, without jargon?
  3. [Likely] Which numbers on your slides are from production, and which are from offline experiments?
  4. [Likely] What part of this work is confidential, and can you describe it at a level that lets us judge it?

L. Stress tests on your architecture diagram (principal-level)

  1. [Likely] Point to the arrow on your diagram where training-serving skew is most likely. How did you detect it, and what contract stops it recurring?
    • Were features or embeddings computed by the same code path offline and online?
  2. [Likely] Your confidence threshold decides what goes to straight-through processing and what goes to a human. Derive that threshold from the cost of a false accept, the cost of a review and the reviewer capacity.
    • What happens to the threshold when a new customer has a different cost of error?
  3. [Likely] Human corrections feed back into training on your diagram. How do you stop that loop from only learning from the cases the model already flagged as uncertain (selection bias)?
    • How do you sample cases the model was confident about but got wrong?
  4. [Likely] If you swap the embedding model behind a retrieval index, what must be rebuilt, how long does it take at your scale, and how do you serve traffic during the migration?
  5. [Likely] Which box on your diagram dominates p99 latency, and which dominates cost? Are they the same box?
    • What would you trade to halve p99 without raising cost?
  6. [Likely] Draw the GPU or accelerator capacity plan for peak load. How do you handle a customer whose batch job arrives at the same time as interactive traffic?
  7. [Likely] Where on your diagram would you add a cascade (cheap model first, expensive model on escalation), and how do you set the escalation rule without hurting recall on rare, costly cases?
  8. [Likely] Which component's output is consumed by another model downstream? How do you stop an upstream model update from silently breaking the downstream one?
    • Who signs off when the upstream team ships a new version?
  9. [Likely] How is each artefact on your diagram versioned (data snapshot, labels, model, prompt, config, index), and can you reproduce last quarter's production output exactly?
  10. [Likely] Your golden set was frozen at launch. How do you know it still represents traffic a year later, and how do you refresh it without making old and new scores incomparable?
  11. [Likely] Show where a single customer with unusual data (one tenant, 40% of volume) would distort your aggregate metrics. How do you report quality so that this is visible?
  12. [Likely] Which metric on your dashboard could improve while customers get worse outcomes? How did you guard against that?
  13. [Likely] If one upstream data source silently changed its schema or encoding, which box would notice first, and how long would bad outputs flow before an alert?
  14. [Likely] What is the blast radius of a bad model release on your diagram, and what mechanisms (canary, per-tenant rollout, kill switch) limit it?
  15. [Likely] Which parts of your system would you keep if the core model were replaced tomorrow by a stronger general foundation model? Which parts are durable advantage?
  16. [Likely] How would your diagram change if each customer needed its own fine-tuned variant? How do you train, store, serve and evaluate thousands of variants?
    • Adapters per tenant, prompt-only customisation, or shared model with retrieval: which and why?
  17. [Likely] Where would an LLM step on your diagram need a deterministic validator (schema, business rule, cross-check against a system of record), and what happens when the validator rejects the output?
  18. [Likely] Walk through one real incident on this system end to end: detection, diagnosis, mitigation and the permanent fix. Which box on the diagram changed afterwards?
  19. [Likely] If you had to run this system with half the team, which boxes would you merge, outsource or delete?
  20. [Likely] How would you prove to a sceptical panel that the research contribution, rather than more data or more compute, caused the headline improvement?
    • What is the compute-matched or data-matched baseline?
  21. [Likely] What would a principal engineer on this panel say is over-engineered on your diagram, and how would you answer?
  22. [Likely] Which design decision on this slide is hardest to reverse, and how much evidence did you have when you made it?

M. Questions to ask the panel (reverse questions)

  1. [Likely] Is the presentation expected to cover my own work, a given topic, or a vision for UiPath, and how is it weighted in the debrief?
  2. [Likely] Who will be on the panel, and is Q&A held to the end or are interruptions expected?
  3. [Likely] Which product area's problems would the person in this role own first?
  4. [Likely] How does UiPath move applied science results into products today, and where does that stall?
  5. [Likely] What does success look like for this role after one year?

03. ML/LLM Science Depth for System Design: Agents, Document Understanding, Screen Understanding, NLP and Evaluation

How to read this chapter. You asked for ML system design, so this chapter covers the science-depth questions that sit underneath design decisions. At principal level, the depth round and the design round overlap: interviewers test whether you can justify each box on the diagram with modelling evidence. Each sub-topic opens with a plain-text reference diagram of the system the questions probe. The diagrams give you a shared picture to reason about. They are not model answers, and they render the same in Markdown and in a PDF export.

Tagging system (same as Chapter 01).

  • [Reported] [n]: a candidate report, question bank or official page links this question, or this round type, to UiPath. The number points to the References list.
  • [Likely]: an inference from the role, the level and UiPath's AI products (agentic automation and Agent Builder, Document Understanding, Communications Mining, Autopilot, the AI Trust Layer, and computer vision for UI automation). No candidate has reported it.
  • Indented sub-bullets are follow-up probes an interviewer may use to push a strong first answer toward the principal bar.

Research limits (please read). Two runs have now tried to research this chapter, and neither could reach a source. In the first run, the shared web-search budget (200 searches) was used up and fetches of glassdoor.com, uipath.com and arxiv.org failed DNS lookup. In the review run (2026-10-14), the search budget was still used up, and the network proxy rejected direct fetches of glassdoor.com, uipath.com, docs.uipath.com, arxiv.org and reddit.com. So no question below is tagged [Reported]. None could be checked against a candidate write-up, and this chapter does not invent reports. Every question is [Likely]. The review run removed two duplicates and added 27 new principal-level [Likely] questions on gaps in the first draft: agent protocols and process conformance, credit assignment, line items and field normalisation, VLM image-token budgets, screen tokenisation and timing, taxonomy discovery, simulated-user evaluation, label noise and privacy-preserving training. The format notes are inferred from how principal-scientist loops usually run at enterprise AI companies; they are not a confirmed UiPath process. A later run with network access can upgrade tags to [Reported] where sources support them.

Format notes (inferred, not confirmed)

Item Likely shape Status
Duration 45-60 min, one or two rounds (ML depth; sometimes merged into research-presentation Q&A) Likely
Interviewer Staff or principal applied scientist from the agents, Document Understanding, Communications Mining or CV/UI team Likely
Style Starts from your past work or a UiPath-shaped problem, then drills down 3-5 levels until you hit the edge of your knowledge Likely
Principal bar (1) You choose a method from first principles and evidence, not fashion. (2) You design evaluation that predicts customer outcomes. (3) You see failure modes before they happen. (4) You know when not to use an LLM. (5) You set direction for a research area across teams. Likely

How depth questions attach to a design diagram

                ┌─────────────────────────── UiPath-shaped ML system ───────────────────────────┐
  Inputs        │  Documents ──> OCR + layout ──> extraction ──> validation ──> RPA / API sink  │
  (docs, email, │  Emails/msgs ─> NLP taxonomy ──> routing ──────────────────────> queue         │
   UI screens,  │  Screens ────> UI element detection ──> grounding ──> agent action ──> robot   │
   logs)        │                         ▲                    ▲                                │
                │                         └──── LLM planner / agent loop (tools, memory) ───────┘
                └──────────────┬────────────────────┬──────────────────────┬───────────────────┘
                               │                    │                      │
                   Depth probe A: Agents   Depth probe B/C: DU & CV   Depth probe D/E: NLP & Eval
                   "why this planner?"     "why this model?"          "how do you know it works?"

Questions

A. Agents and LLM reasoning

   Goal ──> ┌──────────┐   plan   ┌───────────┐  tool call  ┌──────────────────────┐
            │ Planner  │ ───────> │ Executor  │ ──────────> │ Tools: robots, APIs, │
            │  (LLM)   │ <─────── │ (LLM/code)│ <────────── │ DU, UI actions, RAG  │
            └────┬─────┘ observe  └─────┬─────┘  result     └──────────────────────┘
                 │                      │
                 ▼                      ▼
          ┌────────────┐        ┌──────────────┐        ┌────────────────────────┐
          │ Memory /   │        │ Guardrails / │ ─────> │ Human escalation (HITL)│
          │ state      │        │ policy check │        └────────────────────────┘
          └────────────┘        └──────────────┘

  1. [Likely] Walk me through how you would decide between a single ReAct-style agent, a plan-then-execute agent, and a fixed workflow with LLM steps for an enterprise process such as invoice dispute handling.

    • What evidence would make you move from the agent design back to a deterministic workflow?
    • How does your answer change when each tool call triggers a real side effect in an ERP system?

  2. [Likely] Why do LLM agents degrade on long-horizon tasks, and which of those causes can be fixed by better modelling rather than better orchestration?

    • How would you measure error compounding across steps?
    • What does a per-step success rate of 95% imply for a 20-step task, and is that model of failure realistic?

  3. [Likely] How would you design the tool interface (schemas, descriptions, granularity) so an LLM selects the correct tool among 200 enterprise tools?

    • When would you use retrieval over tool descriptions instead of putting all tools in context?
    • How would you evaluate tool-selection accuracy separately from argument-filling accuracy?

  4. [Likely] Compare chain-of-thought prompting, self-consistency, tree search and trained reasoning models for agent planning. Which would you ship under a fixed cost per run?

    • How would you show that extra test-time compute actually improves task success in your domain?

  5. [Likely] How do you make an agent recover from a failed tool call without looping or hallucinating success?

    • How would you detect "fake completion", where the agent says it finished but the system state disagrees?

  6. [Likely] What memory architecture would you use for an agent that runs the same business process thousands of times a day?

    • What should be stored as episodic memory versus distilled into prompts, rules or fine-tuning data?
    • How do you stop memory from one tenant leaking into another?

  7. [Likely] When would you fine-tune a smaller model for agent actions instead of prompting a frontier model? Describe the data you would need.

    • How would you build training trajectories from existing RPA logs and human corrections?
    • What failure modes do you expect from imitation learning on successful trajectories only?

  8. [Likely] How would you apply reinforcement learning (for example, RL from task-level rewards) to improve an enterprise agent, given that real environments are expensive and have side effects?

    • How would you build a safe simulated environment, and how would you check the sim-to-real gap?
    • How would you prevent reward hacking when the reward is "process completed"?

  9. [Likely] Describe a multi-agent design you would build for a cross-system process, and argue whether multiple agents are actually better than one.

    • How do you assign accountability for a failure in a multi-agent run?

  10. [Likely] How would you defend an agent against prompt injection delivered through documents, emails or web pages it reads?

    • What can be done at the model level, and what must be done at the system and permission level?
    • How would you red-team this before release?

  11. [Likely] How would you decide which agent actions need human approval, and how would you set that threshold from data?

    • How would you calibrate the agent's confidence so that the approval policy is trustworthy?

  12. [Likely] How do structured outputs, constrained decoding and function calling change error rates in agents? Where do they fail?

  13. [Likely] How would you blend deterministic RPA robots with LLM agents so that the robot does the reliable parts and the agent handles exceptions?

    • How would the agent hand control back to a robot mid-process?

  14. [Likely] What are the main open research problems in enterprise agents today? If you joined UiPath and could fund one science bet across agents, document understanding and screen understanding, which would it be?

    • How would you staff it, and what would you stop doing to pay for it?
    • How would you know after six months whether that bet was paying off?

  15. [Likely] UiPath exposes robots and agents to other vendors' agents through open protocols such as the Model Context Protocol. How would you design and evaluate tool contracts so that an external agent cannot misuse a UiPath tool?

    • How would you version a tool schema without silently breaking agents that were tuned on the old one?

  16. [Likely] How would you combine a BPMN-style process model with LLM agents so that each agent run can be checked for conformance against the model?

    • What do you do when the agent finds a better path than the modelled one: flag it, block it or learn from it?

  17. [Likely] Given process-mining event logs from a customer, how would you estimate which steps an agent should take over, and the expected value, before anything is built?

    • How would you validate that estimate after deployment?

  18. [Likely] How would you estimate uncertainty for an LLM agent's next action (for example, sampling-based agreement, semantic entropy or a trained verifier), and which would you trust for an approval gate?

  19. [Likely] How would you assign credit to individual steps when training an agent from sparse, trajectory-level success signals?

    • Compare outcome reward models with process reward models for this setting.

  20. [Likely] Agent latency is dominated by sequential LLM calls. Which modelling changes, not only infrastructure changes, would you make to cut end-to-end latency by half?

B. Document understanding: OCR, layout models and extraction

  Scan/PDF/photo
      │
      ▼
 ┌──────────┐   ┌───────────────┐   ┌──────────────┐   ┌────────────────┐   ┌───────────┐
 │ Pre-proc │──>│ OCR / text    │──>│ Classify doc │──>│ Extract fields │──>│ Validate  │──> Export
 │ deskew,  │   │ + boxes, or   │   │ type, split  │   │ (layout model  │   │ rules +   │   to RPA /
 │ denoise  │   │ native PDF    │   │ multi-doc    │   │  or VLM / LLM) │   │ confidence│   ERP
 └──────────┘   └───────────────┘   └──────────────┘   └───────┬────────┘   └─────┬─────┘
                                                               │                  │ low conf.
                                                               │                  ▼
                                                               │          ┌────────────────┐
                                                               └──<───────│ Human validation│
                                                              retrain     │ station (labels)│
                                                                          └────────────────┘

  1. [Likely] Compare an OCR-plus-layout-transformer pipeline (LayoutLM-style) with an OCR-free vision-language model (Donut or a general VLM) for invoice extraction. Which would you ship, and for which customers?

    • How does each behave on low-quality scans, handwriting and 30-page documents?
    • What is the cost per page of each at a million pages a day?

  2. [Likely] How do layout-aware models encode 2D position, and why does that help over plain text sequences?

    • What breaks when the OCR reading order is wrong?

  3. [Likely] How would you handle tables that span pages, have merged cells or have no ruling lines?

    • How would you evaluate table extraction: cell-level, row-level or by downstream task success?

  4. [Likely] How would you produce well-calibrated per-field confidence scores from an extraction model, including an LLM that does not expose useful probabilities?

    • How would you set thresholds that trade straight-through processing rate against error rate per customer?

  5. [Likely] A customer brings a new document type with 20 labelled examples. How would you get to production accuracy fastest?

    • Compare few-shot LLM prompting, fine-tuning a specialist model and active learning.
    • How would you decide when the specialist model should replace the LLM?

  6. [Likely] How would you use human validation corrections as training data without amplifying annotator errors or bias?

    • How would you detect that validators are rubber-stamping low-confidence predictions?

  7. [Likely] How do generative extractors hallucinate values that are not in the document, and how would you detect and prevent this?

    • How would you ground every extracted value back to a region on the page?

  8. [Likely] How would you design a multilingual and multi-script document model, including right-to-left and CJK scripts?

    • Where would you expect the biggest accuracy drops, and how would you find them before customers do?

  9. [Likely] How would you classify and split a 200-page PDF that contains many concatenated documents?

  10. [Likely] How would you train a general-purpose extraction foundation model for business documents? Describe data, objectives and evaluation.

    • How would you get pretraining data while respecting customer data rights?
    • How would you show it beats a frontier VLM on your benchmark and not only on public ones such as DocVQA or FUNSD?

  11. [Likely] What is the role of synthetic documents in training, and how do you check that synthetic gains transfer to real documents?

  12. [Likely] How would you handle handwriting, checkboxes, signatures and stamps in forms?

  13. [Likely] How would you detect drift when a vendor changes its invoice template, and what should happen automatically?

  14. [Likely] How would you distil a large VLM extractor into a model that can run on-premises on CPU?

    • Which accuracy loss would you accept, and how would you explain it to a customer?

  15. [Likely] How would you extract line items and other nested or repeated structures, and link each value to the right key and row?

    • How would you score a line-item prediction where one row is split into two?

  16. [Likely] How do you define 'correct' for an extracted field when dates, amounts, names and addresses can be written many ways? Design the normalisation and the metric.

  17. [Likely] How would you measure how much OCR error propagates into extraction error, and decide whether to spend the next quarter on OCR or on the extractor?

  18. [Likely] A customer uploads documents with no schema. How would you propose a field schema automatically, and how would you evaluate the proposal?

  19. [Likely] A VLM must read a 40-page document at enough resolution to see small print. How would you manage the image-token budget (page selection, tiling, cropping, multi-pass reading)?

    • How would you check that the model did not skip the page that held the answer?

C. Computer vision for UI and screen understanding

  Screenshot (+ optional DOM / accessibility tree)
      │
      ▼
 ┌──────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────┐   ┌─────────────────┐
 │ UI element       │──>│ Element text /   │──>│ Grounding: map an     │──>│ Action: click,  │
 │ detection (boxes,│   │ icon semantics   │   │ instruction or step to│   │ type, select    │
 │ types)           │   │ (OCR + captions) │   │ a target element      │   │ + verify result │
 └──────────────────┘   └──────────────────┘   └──────────┬───────────┘   └────────┬────────┘
                                                          │ anchors / selectors     │ screen diff
                                                          ▼                         ▼
                                                 ┌───────────────────┐     ┌─────────────────┐
                                                 │ Robust re-locate  │<────│ Self-healing on │
                                                 │ on UI changes     │     │ failure         │
                                                 └───────────────────┘     └─────────────────┘

  1. [Likely] How would you build a model that finds UI elements on screens of applications with no accessible DOM, such as Citrix or remote desktop sessions?

    • Detector plus OCR, or an end-to-end VLM? Argue both sides.
    • How do you handle tiny icons, dense tables and high-DPI screens?

  2. [Likely] How would you ground a natural-language step ("click the Submit button in the payment dialog") to the correct element when there are several similar candidates?

    • How do you use spatial context, text anchors and visual similarity together?

  3. [Likely] Selectors break when an application UI is updated. How would you design a self-healing targeting model, and how would you measure that it heals correctly rather than clicking the wrong element?

    • What is the cost of a false heal compared with a failed run, and how does that set your threshold?

  4. [Likely] How would you build a training dataset for screen understanding across thousands of enterprise applications?

    • How would you use synthetic UI rendering, recorded robot runs and weak labels from accessibility trees?
    • How would you remove sensitive data from screenshots used for training?

  5. [Likely] Compare pixel-only computer-use agents with agents that use the DOM or accessibility tree. When does each win?

    • How would you fuse both signals when they disagree?

  6. [Likely] How would you evaluate a computer-use agent? Discuss benchmarks such as OSWorld, WebArena or ScreenSpot and their limits for enterprise apps.

    • What would an internal benchmark look like, and how would you keep it from leaking into training data?

  7. [Likely] How would you verify that a UI action had the intended effect, using only the next screenshot?

  8. [Likely] How would you make screen-understanding inference fast enough for interactive automation on a user's laptop?

    • Which parts would you cache across frames?

  9. [Likely] How would you handle the long tail: custom controls, canvas-rendered apps, legacy terminals and mainframe green screens?

  10. [Likely] How would you turn a recorded user session (task mining) into a reusable automation or agent skill?

    • How do you segment a long recording into steps, and how do you evaluate the segmentation?

  11. [Likely] Screenshots are high-resolution and UI targets are tiny. How would you tokenise or crop screens for a VLM without losing small elements, and how would you test that choice?

  12. [Likely] How would you get a screen model trained mostly on web and Windows apps to generalise to SAP GUI, Java Swing, mobile and terminal UIs?

    • How would you measure the domain gap before collecting new data?

  13. [Likely] How would a UI agent know when the screen is ready to act on (loading spinners, delayed pop-ups, animations), and how would you evaluate timing errors separately from targeting errors?

  14. [Likely] From a single human demonstration, how would you build an automation that generalises to new inputs and slightly different screens?

    • What should the system ask the user to clarify, and when?

D. NLP: communications mining, classification and retrieval

  Emails / tickets / chats
      │
      ▼
 ┌────────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐   ┌───────────────────┐   ┌───────────────────┐
 │ Clean,     │──>│ Multi-label      │──>│ Entity / field    │──>│ Route / trigger    │
 │ thread,    │   │ intent taxonomy  │   │ extraction        │   │ automation, reply  │
 │ dedupe     │   │ (hierarchical)   │   │                   │   │ drafting (RAG)     │
 └────────────┘   └────────┬─────────┘   └───────────────────┘   └───────────────────┘
                           │
                  ┌────────▼─────────┐
                  │ Active learning: │  analysts label → taxonomy evolves → model retrains
                  │ discover, label, │
                  │ refine taxonomy  │
                  └──────────────────┘
  1. [Likely] How would you build a multi-label, hierarchical intent classifier for customer emails where the taxonomy is defined by business users and changes every month?
    • How do you handle labels being split, merged or renamed without relabelling everything?
  2. [Likely] Compare a fine-tuned encoder classifier with zero-shot or few-shot LLM classification on cost, latency, calibration and accuracy at 10 million messages a month.
  3. [Likely] How would you design the active-learning loop so business analysts can build a taxonomy from unlabelled data in a few hours?
    • Which sampling strategy would you use for discovery versus refinement?
    • How do you avoid sampling bias in the resulting evaluation set?
  4. [Likely] How would you handle email threads, quoted replies, signatures and attachments when modelling intent?
  5. [Likely] How would you measure and improve calibration for multi-label predictions so thresholds work per label?
  6. [Likely] How would you design retrieval-augmented generation over enterprise knowledge (process documents, SOPs, policies) for an agent?
    • Which chunking strategy suits SOPs with tables and numbered steps?
    • When do hybrid search, reranking or query rewriting pay for themselves?
    • How do you enforce document-level permissions in retrieval?
  7. [Likely] How would you detect sentiment, urgency and churn risk in operational messages, and how would you prove the signal is useful to the business?
  8. [Likely] How would you support many languages for communications mining with labels mostly in English?
  9. [Likely] How would you mask PII in text before it reaches a third-party LLM while keeping enough context for the task?
    • How do you measure the accuracy loss caused by masking?
  10. [Likely] How would you use LLMs to discover a taxonomy from unlabelled messages (clustering plus LLM-written label names), and how would you evaluate a taxonomy when there is no ground truth?
  11. [Likely] How would you evaluate drafted email replies for factual grounding in the retrieved policy, tone and resolution rate, and which of these can be automated?

E. Evaluation and measurement science

 ┌──────────────┐   ┌─────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────┐   ┌─────────────────────┐
 │ Golden sets  │──>│ Offline metrics     │──>│ Pre-release gates    │──>│ Online monitoring    │
 │ per task /   │   │ field F1, task      │   │ regression suite,    │   │ STP rate, HITL rate, │
 │ tenant slice │   │ success, LLM-judge  │   │ red-team, safety     │   │ cost/run, drift      │
 └──────▲───────┘   └─────────────────────┘   └──────────────────────┘   └──────────┬──────────┘
        │                                                                           │
        └────────────────── human corrections and incidents become new tests ───────┘

  1. [Likely] Design an evaluation framework for enterprise agents that a dozen product teams can share.

    • What is the unit of evaluation: step, trajectory or business outcome?
    • How do you score partial success?

  2. [Likely] How would you validate an LLM-as-judge before relying on it for release decisions?

    • How do you measure and correct position bias, verbosity bias and self-preference?
    • How much human agreement data is enough?

  3. [Likely] Offline field-level F1 went up, but customers report more errors. List possible causes and how you would check each.

  4. [Likely] How would you build test sets that represent customers you cannot see, because their data stays in their tenant?

    • What can privacy-preserving or federated evaluation offer here, and what does it cost?

  5. [Likely] How would you compute confidence intervals and test significance for agent task success when runs are non-deterministic?

    • How many runs per task would you need to detect a 2-point improvement?

  6. [Likely] How would you tie model metrics to business metrics such as straight-through processing rate, handling time saved and cost per transaction?

  7. [Likely] How would you catch benchmark contamination when you use third-party foundation models?

  8. [Likely] How would you design an A/B test or staged rollout for a model change in an automation product, where errors cause downstream financial actions?

    • When would you use shadow mode instead of an A/B test?

  9. [Likely] How would you evaluate safety and policy compliance (for example, the agent must never approve a payment above a limit) in a way that gives strong guarantees, not only averages?

  10. [Likely] How would you evaluate hallucination in extraction and in generated replies, and what metric would you report to a customer?

  11. [Likely] How would you decide that a model is good enough to switch a customer from human validation of every document to sampled validation?

  12. [Likely] How would you compare foundation-model providers for UiPath products when the AI Trust Layer lets customers choose their own model?

    • How do you keep evaluations stable when providers update models silently?

  13. [Likely] How would you evaluate a conversational agent that needs several turns with a user, for example with simulated users as in tau-bench? What makes a simulated user trustworthy?

  14. [Likely] Your golden set has label noise and annotators disagree. How would you measure inter-annotator agreement, fix the set and report metrics that account for the noise?

  15. [Likely] How would you design an internal benchmark that stays useful as models improve, rather than saturating within a year?

  16. [Likely] Which metric would you use to measure human effort saved (for example, time to validate, corrections per document) rather than model accuracy, and how would you collect it without slowing users down?

  17. [Likely] How would you compare verbalised confidence, token log-probabilities and a separate verifier as confidence signals for LLM outputs? Which would you ship?

F. Model strategy, efficiency and deployment science

  1. [Likely] How would you decide between building UiPath-specific models (for documents, communications or UI) and relying on third-party frontier models?

    • What would change your decision in 12 months?

  2. [Likely] How would you route requests between small and large models to minimise cost at fixed quality?

    • How would you train and evaluate the router?

  3. [Likely] Which compression methods (quantisation, distillation, pruning, speculative decoding) would you apply for on-premises deployments, and how do you measure quality loss on the long tail?

  4. [Likely] How would you fine-tune a model per customer (for example, with LoRA adapters) and serve thousands of adapters efficiently?

    • How do you stop one customer's adapter from learning another customer's data?

  5. [Likely] How would you handle continual learning from customer feedback without catastrophic forgetting or silent regressions?

  6. [Likely] How do context length and long-context models change document and agent design, and where do they still fail (for example, lost-in-the-middle)?

  7. [Likely] How would you design a model that is robust to adversarial or malformed documents and screenshots?

  8. [Likely] What is your approach to data governance for training: consent, retention, opt-out and the right to be forgotten in trained models?

  9. [Likely] When would you train one generalist model across documents, communications and UI, and when separate specialists? How would you detect negative transfer between tasks?

  10. [Likely] How would you use LLM-generated synthetic data to train smaller models without collapsing diversity or copying the teacher's errors?

  11. [Likely] When would you apply differential privacy or other formal privacy methods when training on customer documents, and how would you explain the accuracy cost?

G. Principal-level depth on your own work

  1. [Likely] Pick the most complex model you shipped. Draw the system, and tell me which design decision you would reverse today and why.

    • What experiment told you it was the wrong decision?

  2. [Likely] Tell me about a time an offline evaluation misled you. What did you change in your evaluation practice afterwards?

  3. [Likely] Describe a research result you chose not to ship. How did you make that call?

  4. [Likely] Which paper from the last two years most changed how you would build agents or document models, and what would you build differently because of it?

    • What is the weakest part of that paper?

  5. [Likely] How have you set the technical direction for a group of scientists working across several products?

    • How did you settle a disagreement between research ambitions and product deadlines?

  6. [Likely] How do you decide whether a problem needs new research or a well-executed application of known methods?

  7. [Likely] I will show you the abstract and main table of a recent agent or document-AI paper. Critique it as if you were deciding whether your team should build on it.

    • Which missing experiment would change your decision?

  8. [Likely] What standards for reproducibility, baselines and ablations would you require before a result from your team reaches a product roadmap?
